Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Hi, doctor. Thank you for fitting me in, I really appreciate it. Am I sitting in the right spot? 

Um... I hope I'm explaining this right, but I came in because... well, I just feel like I've got this motor running inside me all day. It's not that I'm trying to be a bother or anything, I promise. It's just, if I have to sit still—like when I'm at church, or waiting out there in the clinic, or even just sitting around at home—I start feeling kind of trapped? Oh, well... I wouldn't say I feel worried or anxious, exactly. It's more like... um, I really hope this doesn't make me sound rude, because I swear I'm trying my best to be respectful when people talk to me. I don't ever want to be a burden on anybody.

Oh, goodness... um... I really, really hate to admit this, because I was always taught to have good manners. I swear I don't ever do it on purpose to be disrespectful. 

But... yes. Sometimes I do. I just get so eager to help or to be part of the conversation, and I kind of... well, I jump right in with an answer before folks are even done talking. Like with the pastor at church, or my cousin, or even the nice lady down at the food pantry. They'll be right in the middle of saying something, and the words just pop right out of my mouth. 

And then, almost right after, I realize I answered the completely wrong thing because I didn't wait to hear the whole question. Or I can see on their face that I made them mad by cutting them off. I always apologize right away, I really do, because I feel just awful about it. 

Is that... does that make sense for what you were asking, doctor? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible person.

Oh, um... that's a tough one. I really try not to let any of it cause too much trouble, you know? I do my best to keep it all under control so I'm not bothering anybody. 

But if I had to say which one is the biggest challenge... I guess... maybe the restlessness? And the blurting things out. I just... Does that sound right? I mean, I hope I'm answering this the way you need me to. I just want to make sure I'm giving you a clear picture without sounding like I'm complaining too much.

Well, um... I guess it happens sometimes, but I really do try my absolute best to pay attention, I promise. It's just... well, like when the folks at the food pantry are going over the list of what's available, or when we're talking about church plans, or even when someone's just explaining the times for a ride... I start out listening real good. I look right at them and try to catch every word. 

But then, somehow... my mind just sort of slips off. I don't even realize it's happening, and suddenly I've missed pieces of what they were saying. Important pieces that I really needed to hear. I always feel so bad asking them to go over it again, because I know they're busy and I don't want to waste their time. 

Compared to the fidgeting and interrupting, though... I'd say the moving around definitely happens more often, or at least it feels like it causes more of a fuss. The losing focus... it's there, but I really hope it doesn't make me seem like I don't care about what people are saying, because I truly do. 

Does that... is that the kind of example you were looking for? I just want to be sure I'm explaining it right for you.

I always apologize right away, I really do, because I feel just awful about it. \n\nIs that... does that make sense for what you were asking, doctor? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible person.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your biggest challenges right now—the things that cause you the most trouble in your day-to-day life—would you say it's more the difficulty paying attention and staying focused, or is it more the restlessness and the impulses to interrupt and move around?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a tough one. I really try not to let any of it cause too much trouble, you know? I do my best to keep it all under control so I'm not bothering anybody. \n\nBut if I had to say which one is the biggest challenge... I guess... maybe the restlessness? And the blurting things out. I just... I hate feeling like I'm stepping on people's toes or being a nuisance when I can't sit still or when I interrupt. The focusing part is hard, too, but I can usually just ask folks to repeat themselves, and they're usually real forgiving about it. But when I interrupt or get antsy, I feel like I'm actively being a bother, and I just can't stand the thought of upsetting anyone. \n\nDoes that sound right? I guess it happens sometimes, but I really do try my absolute best to pay attention, I promise. It's just... well, like when the folks at the food pantry are going over the list of what's available, or when we're talking about church plans, or even when someone's just explaining the times for a ride... I start out listening real good. I look right at them and try to catch every word. \n\nBut then, somehow... my mind just sort of slips off. I don't even realize it's happening, and suddenly I've missed pieces of what they were saying. Important pieces that I really needed to hear. I always feel so bad asking them to go over it again, because I know they're busy and I don't want to waste their time. \n\nCompared to the fidgeting and interrupting, though... I'd say the moving around definitely happens more often, or at least it feels like it causes more of a fuss. The losing focus... it's there, but I really hope it doesn't make me seem like I don't care about what people are saying, because I truly do. \n\nDoes that... is that the kind of example you were looking for? Oh, um... I really try my best to get everything done that folks ask of me, I really do. I hate to let anybody down. But... well, I guess it does happen more than I'd like to admit. I don't know an exact number of times a day, but it's enough to make me feel pretty bad about it.

I don't know, my mind just goes somewhere else, and I end up completely forgetting the rest of it. I don't ever leave it half-done on purpose, I swear. But then people find out later that I never actually finished what I started, and I have to apologize all over again. I just worry so much that they'll think I'm lazy or that I don't care about helping them, but it's just that the rest of the job slipped right out of my head. 

I hope that answers your question okay, doctor. I'm trying to be as honest as I can without making it sound like I'm completely unreliable. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?

I hope that's an okay reason to be here.\n\nDoctor: When you have this restless feeling and need to move around, do you also find it hard to focus or keep your attention on one thing, or do you feel more worried and anxious?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't say I feel worried or anxious, exactly. It's more like... um, I really hope this doesn't make me sound rude, because I swear I'm trying my best to be respectful when people talk to me. I don't ever want to be a burden on anybody. \n\nBut, like, when folks are going over things with me... you know, like ride times, or what we need on the pantry list, or plans for church... I listen real good at the start. I really do. But then... I don't know, my mind just sort of slips off somewhere else without me meaning it to. And then I end up missing pieces of what they said, pieces I really needed to hear. \n\nI always feel awful about it. Is that... does that answer what you were asking, doctor? I hope I'm not making a mess of explaining things.\n\nDoctor: How far back do you remember having these troubles with sitting still and keeping your attention? Did this start when you were younger, like in school, or is this something more recent that's come up in your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it's been going on for a real long time. I don't want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything, because I really did try to be good for my teachers. I always wanted them to be happy with me. \n\nBut, back when I was a boy in school... well, the teachers, they were always having to move my seat around. I just... I had a really hard time staying put in my chair. And sometimes I'd talk when I wasn't supposed to, or I'd get a little too eager and jump up before the class was officially over. I'd get in trouble for it, and I always felt just terrible afterwards. I never meant to be disruptive, I promise. \n\nI hope that's the kind of information you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned jumping up before class was over and talking out of turn. Besides those things, do you find yourself doing things without thinking first—like interrupting people, making quick decisions you later regret, or acting on impulses—and does this happen pretty often in your daily life now?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... um... I really, really hate to admit this, because I was always taught to have good manners. I swear I don't ever do it on purpose to be disrespectful. \n\nBut... yes. Sometimes I do. I just get so eager to help or to be part of the conversation, and I kind of... well, I jump right in with an answer before folks are even done talking. Like with the pastor at church, or my cousin, or even the nice lady down at the food pantry. They'll be right in the middle of saying something, and the words just pop right out of my mouth. \n\nAnd then, almost right after, I realize I answered the completely wrong thing because I didn't wait to hear the whole question. Or I can see on their face that I made them mad by cutting them off. I always apologize right away, I really do, because I feel just awful about it. \n\nIs that... does that make sense for what you were asking, doctor? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible person.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your biggest challenges right now—the things that cause you the most trouble in your day-to-day life—would you say it's more the difficulty paying attention and staying focused, or is it more the restlessness and the impulses to interrupt and move around?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a tough one. I really try not to let any of it cause too much trouble, you know? I do my best to keep it all under control so I'm not bothering anybody. \n\nBut if I had to say which one is the biggest challenge... I guess... maybe the restlessness? And the blurting things out. I just... I hate feeling like I'm stepping on people's toes or being a nuisance when I can't sit still or when I interrupt. The focusing part is hard, too, but I can usually just ask folks to repeat themselves, and they're usually real forgiving about it. But when I interrupt or get antsy, I feel like I'm actively being a bother, and I just can't stand the thought of upsetting anyone. \n\nDoes that sound right? I mean, I hope I'm answering this the way you need me to. I just want to make sure I'm giving you a clear picture without sounding like I'm complaining too much.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to do something that requires you to concentrate—like reading a letter, following instructions, or working on a task—how often would you say you actually lose focus or have trouble keeping your mind on it, compared to how often you feel the need to move around or interrupt?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it happens sometimes, but I really do try my absolute best to pay attention, I promise. I start out listening real good. I look right at them and try to catch every word. \n\nBut then, somehow... my mind just sort of slips off. I don't even realize it's happening, and suddenly I've missed pieces of what they were saying. Important pieces that I really needed to hear. I just want to be sure I'm explaining it right for you.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home or at work, on a typical day, how many times would you say you have trouble finishing something you started—like not completing a task, a chore, or even a conversation—because your mind wandered or you got distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I don't know an exact number of times a day, but it's enough to make me feel pretty bad about it. \n\nLike, I'll start an errand for my relative, or I'll be given a job to do down at the pantry. And I always start off real strong, you know? I'll get the very first part done just fine. But then... I don't know, my mind just goes somewhere else, and I end up completely forgetting the rest of it. I don't ever leave it half-done on purpose, I swear. But then people find out later that I never actually finished what I started, and I have to apologize all over again. I'm trying to be as honest as I can without making it sound like I'm completely unreliable. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that the restlessness and interrupting bother you more, but I want to make sure I understand the full picture. Did this start when you were younger, like in school, or is this something more recent that's come up in your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it's been going on for a real long time. I don't want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything, because I really did try to be good for my teachers. I always wanted them to be happy with me. \n\nBut, back when I was a boy in school... well, the teachers, they were always having to move my seat around. I just... I had a really hard time staying put in my chair. And sometimes I'd talk when I wasn't supposed to, or I'd get a little too eager and jump up before the class was officially over. I'd get in trouble for it, and I always felt just terrible afterwards. I never meant to be disruptive, I promise. \n\nI hope that's the kind of information you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned jumping up before class was over and talking out of turn. Besides those things, do you find yourself doing things without thinking first—like interrupting people, making quick decisions you later regret, or acting on impulses—and does this happen pretty often in your daily life now?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... um... I really, really hate to admit this, because I was always taught to have good manners. I swear I don't ever do it on purpose to be disrespectful. \n\nBut... yes. Sometimes I do. I just get so eager to help or to be part of the conversation, and I kind of... well, I jump right in with an answer before folks are even done talking. Like with the pastor at church, or my cousin, or even the nice lady down at the food pantry. They'll be right in the middle of saying something, and the words just pop right out of my mouth. \n\nAnd then, almost right after, I realize I answered the completely wrong thing because I didn't wait to hear the whole question. Or I can see on their face that I made them mad by cutting them off. I always apologize right away, I really do, because I feel just awful about it. \n\nIs that... does that make sense for what you were asking, doctor? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible person.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your biggest challenges right now—the things that cause you the most trouble in your day-to-day life—would you say it's more the difficulty paying attention and staying focused, or is it more the restlessness and the impulses to interrupt and move around?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a tough one. I really try not to let any of it cause too much trouble, you know? I do my best to keep it all under control so I'm not bothering anybody. \n\nBut if I had to say which one is the biggest challenge... I guess... maybe the restlessness? And the blurting things out. I just... I hate feeling like I'm stepping on people's toes or being a nuisance when I can't sit still or when I interrupt. The focusing part is hard, too, but I can usually just ask folks to repeat themselves, and they're usually real forgiving about it. But when I interrupt or get antsy, I feel like I'm actively being a bother, and I just can't stand the thought of upsetting anyone. \n\nDoes that sound right? I mean, I hope I'm answering this the way you need me to. I just want to make sure I'm giving you a clear picture without sounding like I'm complaining too much.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to do something that requires you to concentrate—like reading a letter, following instructions, or working on a task—how often would you say you actually lose focus or have trouble keeping your mind on it, compared to how often you feel the need to move around or interrupt?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it happens sometimes, but I really do try my absolute best to pay attention, I promise. It's just... well, like when the folks at the food pantry are going over the list of what's available, or when we're talking about church plans, or even when someone's just explaining the times for a ride... I start out listening real good. I look right at them and try to catch every word. \n\nBut then, somehow... my mind just sort of slips off. I don't even realize it's happening, and suddenly I've missed pieces of what they were saying. Important pieces that I really needed to hear. I always feel so bad asking them to go over it again, because I know they're busy and I don't want to waste their time. \n\nCompared to the fidgeting and interrupting, though... I'd say the moving around definitely happens more often, or at least it feels like it causes more of a fuss. The losing focus... it's there, but I really hope it doesn't make me seem like I don't care about what people are saying, because I truly do. \n\nDoes that... is that the kind of example you were looking for? I just want to be sure I'm explaining it right for you.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home or at work, on a typical day, how many times would you say you have trouble finishing something you started—like not completing a task, a chore, or even a conversation—because your mind wandered or you got distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I really try my best to get everything done that folks ask of me, I really do. I hate to let anybody down. But... well, I guess it does happen more than I'd like to admit. I don't know an exact number of times a day, but it's enough to make me feel pretty bad about it. \n\nLike, I'll start an errand for my relative, or I'll be given a job to do down at the pantry. And I always start off real strong, you know? I'll get the very first part done just fine. But then... I don't know, my mind just goes somewhere else, and I end up completely forgetting the rest of it. I don't ever leave it half-done on purpose, I swear. But then people find out later that I never actually finished what I started, and I have to apologize all over again. I just worry so much that they'll think I'm lazy or that I don't care about helping them, but it's just that the rest of the job slipped right out of my head. \n\nI hope that answers your question okay, doctor. I'm trying to be as honest as I can without making it sound like I'm completely unreliable. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that the restlessness and interrupting bother you more, but I want to make sure I understand the full picture. On a typical day right now, would you say you have more trouble with sitting still and the urge to move around, or do you have more trouble with things like losing track of what you're doing, forgetting tasks, or having a hard time organizing things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really do try to keep everything straight, I promise I do. I don't want you to think I'm just letting things fall apart on purpose. I do my very best to keep track of it all.\n\nBut... if I'm being completely honest, the organizing part... it's a real struggle for me. I guess maybe it is just as bad as the moving around, if I really think about it. It's just... well, my papers, my appointment cards, the food boxes from the pantry, the little notes I try to keep on my phone... they all just get so mixed up. I look at it all, and I just can't figure out what needs doing first. And then, before I know it, everything is already late or I've messed something up entirely. \n\nI hate it, I really do. I feel awful when I miss an appointment or mix up a box because I couldn't get my things in order. I try so hard to be helpful and stay on top of things, but it just gets so jumbled up in my head and in my space. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely helpless, because I really do try. Is that what you were asking about, doctor?\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, would you say the trouble with organizing things, losing track of tasks, and forgetting what you need to do causes you just as much difficulty in your daily life as the fidgeting and interrupting, or does one of them really cause you more problems than the other?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess they both just kind of pile up and cause a lot of trouble. I really hate to admit this, because I try so hard to be a good helper, I really do. But... if I'm being completely honest... it's gotten to where folks at church, and down at the pantry, and even my own family... they've sort of stopped trusting me with things. \n\nLike, they don't ask me to handle the food supplies anymore, or help out with the rides, or keep up with the appointments. I think all the forgetting and the jumping around just got to be too much for them to rely on me. I feel like I just keep letting everybody down, even when I truly, truly mean to help and do a good job. I promise I don't do it on purpose, it just... slips away from me. \n\nI don't want you to think I'm a bad person or that I don't care. I just mess it up despite trying my absolute best. Did I explain that okay for you, doctor? I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you need.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Well, um... I guess if I had to pick which one happens more often... maybe it's the losing focus part? I really try to pay attention, I promise I do. I want to be respectful and listen to people.

I really try my best to get everything done that folks ask of me, I really do. I hate to let anybody down. But... well, I guess it does happen more than I'd like to admit. I don't know an exact number of times a day, but it's enough to make me feel pretty bad about it. \n\nLike, I'll start an errand for my relative, or I'll be given a job to do down at the pantry. I don't know, my mind just goes somewhere else, and I end up completely forgetting the rest of it. I don't ever leave it half-done on purpose, I swear. But then people find out later that I never actually finished what I started, and I have to apologize all over again. I just worry so much that they'll think I'm lazy or that I don't care about helping them, but it's just that the rest of the job slipped right out of my head. \n\nI hope that answers your question okay, doctor. I feel like I just keep letting everybody down, even when I truly, truly mean to help and do a good job. I promise I don't do it on purpose, it just... slips away from me. \n\nI don't want you to think I'm a bad person or that I don't care. I just mess it up despite trying my absolute best. Did I explain that okay for you, doctor? I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you need.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at your whole day—from morning to night—do you spend more time dealing with the fidgeting and needing to move around and interrupt people, or do you spend more time struggling with losing focus, forgetting things, and getting disorganized?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... wow, looking at the whole day... that's a tough one to measure, I guess. I really try to just take it as it comes and do my best, you know? \n\nI... I honestly don't know if I spend more hours on one or the other. It's sort of like they're both just there all the time, kind of mixed together. Like, I might be moving around too much while I'm also forgetting what I was supposed to be doing. I haven't really kept a clock on which one takes up more of my day, if that makes sense. \n\nI'm sorry I can't give you a better answer on which one is worse time-wise. I hope that's okay, doctor. I really wish I could be more specific for you... is it alright if I just don't know for sure?\n\nDoctor: When you think about a typical day, do you find yourself more often losing focus and forgetting things, or more often feeling restless and interrupting people?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I had to pick which one happens more often... maybe it's the losing focus part? I really try to pay attention, I promise I do. I want to be respectful and listen to people. \n\nBut... like, when the folks at church are talking through the ride times, or we're going over the pantry lists, or figuring out the plans for the week... I start out listening real good. I'm right there with them. But then... I don't know, my mind just sort of slips off somewhere else. And before I know it, I've missed pieces of what they were saying, things I really needed to hear to do my job right. \n\nI feel so bad when I have to ask them to repeat it, or when I mess up because I missed a detail. I don't want them to think I don't care about what they're saying, because I really do. I just... I can't seem to hold onto it for very long. \n\nDoes that answer your question better, doctor? I hope I'm giving you what you need to know. I'm really trying to be helpful.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou've mentioned both the trouble focusing and the restlessness. When you're sitting down trying to do something that needs your full attention—like reading something important or following step-by-step instructions—how often during a typical week would you say you struggle to keep your mind on it, compared to how often you feel that urge to get up and move around?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Leaving_Seat\nmanifestation: \"In meetings, meals, and waiting rooms, I keep standing up to get water, check outside, or move around, even when everybody else is sitting and looking at me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um... when it comes to sitting down for something important, I... I guess the moving around part happens a whole lot. Maybe even more than the focus part, if I really think about it. 

I try so hard to just sit still and be good, especially when I'm supposed to be paying attention. But... like, if I'm in a meeting at church, or even just sitting at the table for a meal, or waiting at the doctor's office... I just get this feeling in my legs. It's like I have to get up. 

I'll make up little excuses, you know? Like, I'll stand up to go get a glass of water, or I'll say I need to check something outside... just so I can move around a little bit. And I know I shouldn't, because everybody else is just sitting there nicely, and then they all look at me... and I feel so embarrassed. I really don't mean to be disruptive or rude. I just can't seem to stay in my chair like a normal person. 

I hope that doesn't make me sound too difficult, doctor. I really am trying to do better with it. Did that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.

I guess they both just kind of pile up and cause a lot of trouble. I really hate to admit this, because I try so hard to be a good helper, I really do. I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you need.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at your whole day—from morning to night—do you spend more time dealing with the fidgeting and needing to move around and interrupt people, or do you spend more time struggling with losing focus, forgetting things, and getting disorganized?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... wow, looking at the whole day... that's a tough one to measure, I guess. I guess it happens sometimes, but I really do try my absolute best to pay attention, I promise. It's just... well, like when the folks at the food pantry are going over the list of what's available, or when we're talking about church plans, or even when someone's just explaining the times for a ride... I start out listening real good. I look right at them and try to catch every word. \n\nBut then, somehow... my mind just sort of slips off. I don't even realize it's happening, and suddenly I've missed pieces of what they were saying. Important pieces that I really needed to hear. I always feel so bad asking them to go over it again, because I know they're busy and I don't want to waste their time. \n\nCompared to the fidgeting and interrupting, though... I'd say the moving around definitely happens more often, or at least it feels like it causes more of a fuss. The losing focus... it's there, but I really hope it doesn't make me seem like I don't care about what people are saying, because I truly do. \n\nDoes that... is that the kind of example you were looking for? I just want to be sure I'm explaining it right for you.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home or at work, on a typical day, how many times would you say you have trouble finishing something you started—like not completing a task, a chore, or even a conversation—because your mind wandered or you got distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I really try my best to get everything done that folks ask of me, I really do. I hate to let anybody down. But... well, I guess it does happen more than I'd like to admit. I don't know an exact number of times a day, but it's enough to make me feel pretty bad about it. \n\nLike, I'll start an errand for my relative, or I'll be given a job to do down at the pantry. And I always start off real strong, you know? I'll get the very first part done just fine. But then... I don't know, my mind just goes somewhere else, and I end up completely forgetting the rest of it. I don't ever leave it half-done on purpose, I swear. But then people find out later that I never actually finished what I started, and I have to apologize all over again. I just worry so much that they'll think I'm lazy or that I don't care about helping them, but it's just that the rest of the job slipped right out of my head. \n\nI hope that answers your question okay, doctor. I'm trying to be as honest as I can without making it sound like I'm completely unreliable. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that the restlessness and interrupting bother you more, but I want to make sure I understand the full picture. On a typical day right now, would you say you have more trouble with sitting still and the urge to move around, or do you have more trouble with things like losing track of what you're doing, forgetting tasks, or having a hard time organizing things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really do try to keep everything straight, I promise I do. I don't want you to think I'm just letting things fall apart on purpose. I do my very best to keep track of it all.\n\nBut... if I'm being completely honest, the organizing part... it's a real struggle for me. I guess maybe it is just as bad as the moving around, if I really think about it. It's just... well, my papers, my appointment cards, the food boxes from the pantry, the little notes I try to keep on my phone... they all just get so mixed up. I look at it all, and I just can't figure out what needs doing first. And then, before I know it, everything is already late or I've messed something up entirely. \n\nI hate it, I really do. I feel awful when I miss an appointment or mix up a box because I couldn't get my things in order. I try so hard to be helpful and stay on top of things, but it just gets so jumbled up in my head and in my space. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely helpless, because I really do try. Is that what you were asking about, doctor?\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, would you say the trouble with organizing things, losing track of tasks, and forgetting what you need to do causes you just as much difficulty in your daily life as the fidgeting and interrupting, or does one of them really cause you more problems than the other?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess they both just kind of pile up and cause a lot of trouble. I really hate to admit this, because I try so hard to be a good helper, I really do. But... if I'm being completely honest... it's gotten to where folks at church, and down at the pantry, and even my own family... they've sort of stopped trusting me with things. \n\nLike, they don't ask me to handle the food supplies anymore, or help out with the rides, or keep up with the appointments. I think all the forgetting and the jumping around just got to be too much for them to rely on me. I feel like I just keep letting everybody down, even when I truly, truly mean to help and do a good job. I promise I don't do it on purpose, it just... slips away from me. \n\nI don't want you to think I'm a bad person or that I don't care. I just mess it up despite trying my absolute best. Did I explain that okay for you, doctor? I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you need.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at your whole day—from morning to night—do you spend more time dealing with the fidgeting and needing to move around and interrupt people, or do you spend more time struggling with losing focus, forgetting things, and getting disorganized?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... wow, looking at the whole day... that's a tough one to measure, I guess. I really try to just take it as it comes and do my best, you know? \n\nI... I honestly don't know if I spend more hours on one or the other. It's sort of like they're both just there all the time, kind of mixed together. Like, I might be moving around too much while I'm also forgetting what I was supposed to be doing. I haven't really kept a clock on which one takes up more of my day, if that makes sense. \n\nI'm sorry I can't give you a better answer on which one is worse time-wise. I hope that's okay, doctor. I really wish I could be more specific for you... is it alright if I just don't know for sure?\n\nDoctor: When you think about a typical day, do you find yourself more often losing focus and forgetting things, or more often feeling restless and interrupting people?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I had to pick which one happens more often... maybe it's the losing focus part? I really try to pay attention, I promise I do. I want to be respectful and listen to people. \n\nBut... like, when the folks at church are talking through the ride times, or we're going over the pantry lists, or figuring out the plans for the week... I start out listening real good. I'm right there with them. But then... I don't know, my mind just sort of slips off somewhere else. And before I know it, I've missed pieces of what they were saying, things I really needed to hear to do my job right. \n\nI feel so bad when I have to ask them to repeat it, or when I mess up because I missed a detail. I don't want them to think I don't care about what they're saying, because I really do. I just... I can't seem to hold onto it for very long. \n\nDoes that answer your question better, doctor? Maybe even more than the focus part, if I really think about it. \n\nI try so hard to just sit still and be good, especially when I'm supposed to be paying attention. It's like I have to get up. \n\nI'll make up little excuses, you know? Like, I'll stand up to go get a glass of water, or I'll say I need to check something outside... just so I can move around a little bit. I just can't seem to stay in my chair like a normal person. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound too difficult, doctor. I really am trying to do better with it. Did that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few months, when you think about what causes you the most problems and gets you into the most trouble—the difficulty paying attention and remembering things, or the restlessness and interrupting—which one would you say is the bigger issue for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... the biggest issue? Oh, um... if I had to pick just one to work on first... I think I'd really want to fix the interrupting part. I mean, I try so hard to be helpful and show everyone I'm engaged, but... well, I end up jumping in with an answer before people are even finished talking.
